Table 6
Generative Large Language Models: Prompting, Applications, Fine-Tuning, and Evaluation
1. Q: What is natural language generation (NLG), and how do LLMs support it?
A: Show answer
Natural language generation is the production of text from an instruction, context, structured input, or conversational state. LLMs support NLG by using learned language representations to generate a sequence that fits the supplied context. The chapter demonstrates creative writing, summarization, dialogue, and question answering, alongside NLP tasks such as sentiment analysis and entity extraction. A generative model can often perform a task through a prompt without task-specific training, but the result remains a prediction rather than a guaranteed correct answer. The quality of NLG depends on the model, prompt, input evidence, decoding settings, evaluation, and safeguards appropriate to the application.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish generating a text response from classifying or extracting information, and identify how each task should be evaluated.
Key point:
- NLG produces text conditioned on input and context.
- Fluent generation still requires task-specific quality checks.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
2. Q: How do LLM-based NLP tasks differ from conventional rule-based or statistical NLP systems?
A: Show answer
Conventional NLP systems often depend on manually designed features, rules, or models trained for a specific task. LLMs are generally pre-trained on broad text data and can respond to natural-language instructions for multiple tasks, including classification, extraction, and topic identification. This flexibility reduces the need to build a separate pipeline for every use case, especially when labeled data is unavailable. However, a specialist model trained for a well-defined task may outperform a generic LLM and may be cheaper, faster, or easier to validate. The chapter therefore treats LLMs as an additional option rather than a universal replacement for task-specific NLP systems.
Study instruction:
- Compare a prompt-based LLM with a task-specific classifier in terms of labeled data, preprocessing, performance, cost, and error control.
Key point:
- LLMs offer broad task flexibility through prompting.
- Specialist models may remain preferable for stable, well-defined tasks.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
3. Q: When might a generic LLM be preferable to training a task-specific NLP model?
A: Show answer
A generic LLM may be useful when there is little or no labeled training data, when incoming text differs from the examples available for a specialist model, or when a task is exploratory and requirements are still changing. Prompting can provide a fast baseline without a separate model-training cycle. It may also be useful for low-volume tasks where the cost of building and maintaining a dedicated model is not justified. The decision should still account for output reliability, privacy, latency, inference cost, and the consequences of errors. If the task is repetitive, high-volume, regulated, or requires consistent structured output, a specialist model or controlled fine-tuning may be more suitable.
Study instruction:
- Identify a use case with limited labels and decide whether prompting is an appropriate prototype or a long-term production approach.
Key point:
- Prompting is useful when labels or training resources are limited.
- Production choice must be based on measured quality and operational risk.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
4. Q: What is prompt engineering, and why does it matter when using an LLM?
A: Show answer
Prompt engineering is the deliberate design and refinement of instructions and context to guide a model toward a desired response. A prompt may specify the task, input text, allowed labels, response format, constraints, and examples. The chapter emphasizes that natural language is expressive but can be ambiguous, and small wording changes may alter the generated result. Prompt engineering is therefore an iterative process: create a baseline, test it on representative cases, inspect failures, and refine the instruction. A prompt should be evaluated as part of the system, not treated as a one-time sentence that guarantees a correct answer.
Study instruction:
- Write two prompts for the same task, vary one instruction at a time, and record how the output changes across a fixed test set.
Key point:
- Prompts shape task interpretation and output behavior.
- Small wording changes can affect consistency and accuracy.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
5. Q: How can an LLM perform sentiment analysis without task-specific fine-tuning?
A: Show answer
The chapter demonstrates a text-generation pipeline in which a prompt asks the model to classify a supplied passage as neutral, negative, or positive. The prompt includes the text and a label cue, and the model generates a short continuation such as “Positive.” This is a zero-shot classification pattern: the model uses its pre-trained and instruction-following capabilities without updating its parameters for the sentiment dataset. It is convenient for prototyping, but the output may be inconsistent or verbose unless the allowed labels and response format are specified. A production classifier should be tested against labeled examples and checked for class-specific errors, ambiguity, sarcasm, and domain-specific language.
Study instruction:
- Create a three-class sentiment prompt and test it on clearly positive, clearly negative, neutral, sarcastic, and mixed examples.
Key point:
- Prompting can turn text generation into a classification interface.
- Zero-shot output still requires labeled evaluation and error review.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
6. Q: What prompt-design practices improve sentiment-analysis results?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends clarity and specificity, relevant contextual cues, follow-up questions for ambiguous cases, sample responses, iterative refinement, and awareness of the chosen model's instruction-following capability. A good prompt defines the permitted classes and states the task directly. Context should be included only when it legitimately affects interpretation; unnecessary background can distract the model. Examples can demonstrate the expected mapping between text and label. For operational use, the prompt should also request a machine-readable or otherwise constrained output and define how uncertainty should be represented. These practices improve consistency but do not remove the need for a representative evaluation set or independent review of consequential classifications.
Study instruction:
- Build a prompt template that specifies labels, context, examples, output format, and a rule for ambiguous sentiment.
Key point:
- Clear instructions and representative examples reduce ambiguity.
- Prompt refinement must be supported by systematic testing.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
7. Q: How should contextual cues be used in sentiment prompts?
A: Show answer
Context can clarify what a statement refers to and how its wording should be interpreted. A product review written by a technical expert may use terms differently from a casual consumer review, and irony or domain-specific vocabulary can reverse the apparent sentiment of individual words. The chapter recommends supplying relevant context where it may influence classification. Context must be factual and proportionate: adding assumptions about the author or product can bias the result. A robust prompt distinguishes the text being classified from optional background and tells the model not to infer sentiment from irrelevant attributes. Test the prompt with and without contextual cues to confirm that context improves rather than distorts the classification.
Study instruction:
- Compare sentiment classifications with and without a relevant context statement and identify when the context changes interpretation appropriately.
Key point:
- Context helps interpret nuanced language.
- Irrelevant or assumed context can introduce bias.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
8. Q: When are follow-up questions useful in prompt-based sentiment analysis?
A: Show answer
Follow-up questions are useful when a passage contains mixed opinions, irony, uncertainty, or unclear emotional direction. The chapter suggests asking whether sentiment is clear or ambiguous and inviting the model to consider subtle cues. This can expose uncertainty that a forced positive, negative, or neutral label would hide. A system should define how it handles mixed sentiment, such as returning an “uncertain” status, requesting human review, or separating sentiment by aspect. Follow-up prompting does not prove that the model has correctly resolved ambiguity; it merely gives it another opportunity to analyze the text. The result should be checked against human-labeled examples and the application's decision rules.
Study instruction:
- Prepare examples with mixed sentiment and define when the model should return an uncertainty flag rather than a single confident label.
Key point:
- Follow-up prompts can surface ambiguity and missing context.
- A clear escalation path is needed when the text cannot be classified reliably.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
9. Q: How do sample responses support few-shot sentiment classification?
A: Show answer
Sample responses demonstrate the expected relationship between input text and output labels. The chapter gives positive and negative review examples and leaves a third example for the model to classify. These demonstrations can teach the model the intended label vocabulary, output style, and level of detail without changing its parameters. Examples should be representative, balanced, and consistent with the actual annotation policy. Poorly selected examples can teach unintended patterns, such as associating a label with a particular position or phrase. The system should be tested on unseen examples and with different example orders to check whether the result depends too heavily on the demonstrations.
Study instruction:
- Create a balanced set of positive, negative, and neutral examples and test whether changing their order alters the model's predictions.
Key point:
- Few-shot examples communicate the desired input-output pattern.
- Example quality and selection can introduce bias.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
10. Q: What is iterative prompt refinement, and how should it be performed?
A: Show answer
Iterative refinement begins with a simple prompt, evaluates its responses, identifies recurring errors, and changes the instruction to address those errors. For sentiment analysis, refinement may specify how to treat sarcasm, ask for the main factors behind a negative rating, or require one label from a fixed list. Changes should be made systematically so the team can determine which revision caused an improvement. A stable evaluation set should be retained to prevent a prompt from being tuned only to a few familiar examples. Prompt versions, test results, and known limitations should be documented so the deployed configuration can be reproduced and rolled back if a later revision performs worse.
Study instruction:
- Maintain a prompt-change log showing the issue, revision, test cases, metric change, and approval decision for each iteration.
Key point:
- Prompt refinement is an evidence-based improvement cycle.
- Version control and repeatable tests prevent accidental regression.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
11. Q: How can an LLM perform entity extraction from text without fine-tuning?
A: Show answer
Entity extraction can be requested directly through a prompt, for example by asking the model to return named entities from a sentence about Mount Everest. The chapter's Falcon example identifies Mount Everest, Earth, and the Himalayas from the supplied passage. This illustrates zero-shot extraction: the model uses its general language knowledge to identify likely entities without task-specific parameter updates. A practical system should define the entity categories, output schema, treatment of repeated mentions, and exact span requirements. If the result is used in a database or workflow, validate the extracted entities against the original text and reject invented entities or unsupported normalization.
Study instruction:
- Ask a model to extract entities from a sample passage, then compare each returned item with the exact source span and required category.
Key point:
- Prompt-based extraction can provide a rapid baseline.
- Exact spans and categories need validation before downstream use.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
12. Q: What limitations should be considered when using a general LLM for entity extraction?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies difficulty with nuanced domain entities, ambiguity, long-tail entities, specialized vocabulary, and newly emerging entities. A general model may miss a drug name or legal term if the term is rare or requires domain knowledge. It may confuse a proper noun with a common word or fail to recognize a new product or person that appeared after its training data. Prompt refinement and contextual priming can help, but fine-tuning on domain-specific examples may be necessary for consistent results. The system should also validate exact spans, entity categories, and relationships, because a plausible extracted label may not be supported by the source text.
Study instruction:
- Build an error set containing rare, ambiguous, newly coined, and domain-specific entities and evaluate the model separately on each group.
Key point:
- General language capability does not guarantee reliable specialist extraction.
- Domain-specific evaluation and adaptation may be required.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
13. Q: How can an LLM be used for topic modeling or main-topic extraction?
A: Show answer
The chapter demonstrates prompting a model to extract the main topic from a paragraph about cybersecurity. The prompt supplies the text and asks for a concise topic label; the model returns a phrase such as “The importance of cybersecurity in the digital age.” This is a semantic summarization of the passage rather than a statistical discovery of topics across a large corpus. It can be useful when a user needs a readable topic description for one document or a small set of documents. For a large corpus, topic consistency, label granularity, repeatability, and cross-document comparability must be assessed. A controlled taxonomy or clustering stage may be needed for systematic reporting.
Study instruction:
- Apply the same topic prompt to several related and unrelated documents and evaluate whether the resulting labels are consistent and sufficiently specific.
Key point:
- Prompt-based topic extraction can produce readable topic descriptions.
- Corpus-level topic analysis requires consistency checks across documents.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
14. Q: How does prompt-based topic extraction compare with Latent Dirichlet Allocation (LDA)?
A: Show answer
LDA is a statistical generative model that identifies topics through word-distribution patterns across a document corpus and generally requires the analyst to specify the number of topics. It can be useful when a training corpus and a similar test corpus are available and consistent statistical grouping is required. An LLM can instead use contextual language representations to produce a concise topic description from a supplied passage, which may be more accessible when labeled data or a stable corpus is unavailable. The approaches solve related but not identical problems. LDA's topic-word distributions and an LLM's generated description should not be compared as if they were the same output; evaluate usefulness, stability, interpretability, and corpus coverage for the intended use.
Study instruction:
- Compare an LDA topic-word list with an LLM-generated topic description for the same document set and identify what each method reveals or obscures.
Key point:
- LDA models word distributions across a corpus; an LLM can generate context-aware topic descriptions.
- Method choice depends on data, scale, consistency, and interpretability requirements.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
15. Q: What makes LLMs useful for creative writing?
A: Show answer
Creative writing requires generating narratives, poems, descriptions, and dialogue that follow a prompt while developing imagery, tone, and structure. The chapter demonstrates prompting Falcon to write a poem about the northern lights. Unlike a classifier that returns a fixed label, a generative model can produce open-ended language and vary its output according to the instruction and decoding settings. The result can serve as a draft, idea generator, or collaborative starting point. It should not be treated as a substitute for editorial judgment: coherence, originality, factual references, voice, audience suitability, and rights or attribution concerns still need review.
Study instruction:
- Generate two different creative drafts from the same prompt and compare imagery, structure, tone, and relevance to the requested subject.
Key point:
- LLMs can generate open-ended creative text from short instructions.
- Human editing remains necessary for quality, originality, and suitability.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
16. Q: How can an LLM be fine-tuned to generate text in a specific genre or style?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends curating high-quality training examples from the desired genre, such as science-fiction texts for science-fiction generation, and combining that data with prompts that specify genre conventions and desired content. Fine-tuning can help a model internalize recurring vocabulary, sentence structures, and thematic patterns represented in the curated material. The dataset should be legally usable, diverse enough to avoid overfitting to a narrow set of examples, and appropriate for the target audience. A prompt may specify a setting or narrative trope, but it should not be used to imitate a living author's distinctive style without appropriate permission. Evaluate outputs for genre fit, originality, repetition, and harmful stereotypes.
Study instruction:
- Define a genre dataset, document its provenance and selection criteria, and create an evaluation rubric for genre consistency and originality.
Key point:
- Curated data and precise prompts can specialize creative generation.
- Data rights, diversity, and style-related safeguards matter.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
17. Q: What is the role of temperature in text generation?
A: Show answer
Temperature adjusts the sharpness of the model's next-token probability distribution. Lower values generally make the model favor higher-probability tokens and produce more conservative, repeatable continuations. Higher values generally spread probability more broadly and can produce more varied or surprising text. The chapter recommends experimenting with temperature when balancing creativity and genre adherence. Temperature does not directly measure quality or factuality, and a higher value does not guarantee originality. The right setting depends on the task: a constrained classification response usually needs less variation than creative writing. Any configuration should be tested for repetition, relevance, stability, and errors on representative prompts.
Study instruction:
- Generate the same prompt at low, medium, and high temperature and compare diversity, consistency, and factual drift.
Key point:
- Temperature influences randomness in token selection.
- Choose settings according to task requirements and evaluate the trade-off.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
18. Q: What is the difference between extractive and abstractive summarization?
A: Show answer
Extractive summarization selects important sentences or passages from the source and combines them, while abstractive summarization generates new wording that expresses the source's main ideas. The chapter emphasizes abstractive summarization as a natural use of generative LLMs. Extractive output is more directly traceable to source passages but may be repetitive or lack smooth transitions. Abstractive output can be concise and readable, but it may omit key qualifications or introduce information not present in the source. The choice depends on the need for readability, traceability, length, and risk. In high-stakes contexts, source-linked extracts or sentence-level evidence may be preferable to a free-form summary alone.
Study instruction:
- Create an extractive and an abstractive summary of the same paragraph and compare traceability, readability, coverage, and factual fidelity.
Key point:
- Extractive summaries select source text; abstractive summaries generate new phrasing.
- Abstractive fluency increases the need for source verification.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
19. Q: How should a prompt be designed for a concise abstractive summary?
A: Show answer
A useful summarization prompt identifies the source text, states that a summary is required, and specifies the intended length, audience, and coverage priorities. The chapter demonstrates setting a maximum number of generated tokens and returning only the generated summary rather than repeating the prompt. A stronger operational prompt also instructs the model to preserve important facts, numbers, uncertainty, and qualifications and not to add unsupported information. If the source contains several sections, the prompt should indicate whether the summary should cover all sections or prioritize particular themes. The output should be checked against the source because token limits can cause omissions and generation can introduce unsupported details.
Study instruction:
- Draft a summary prompt with a length limit, audience, required facts, and an explicit prohibition on adding information not present in the source.
Key point:
- Prompt constraints define summary scope and output length.
- Source comparison is required to confirm coverage and fidelity.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
20. Q: What are hallucinations in LLM-generated summaries, and why are they dangerous?
A: Show answer
A hallucination occurs when a model generates a plausible statement that is not supported by the source or is factually incorrect. In summarization, this may involve inventing a result, changing a number, overstating a conclusion, or adding background information that the document did not contain. The chapter warns that fluent summaries can distort facts and recommends comparing generated output with the original text. The risk is particularly serious when summaries inform medical, legal, financial, policy, or research decisions. A summary should preserve the source's uncertainty and limits, and reviewers should be able to trace important claims to their supporting passages. Deterministic decoding alone does not guarantee factuality.
Study instruction:
- Review a generated summary sentence by sentence and classify each claim as supported, contradicted, omitted, or not found in the source.
Key point:
- A coherent summary can contain unsupported or false statements.
- Factual verification must compare output with source evidence.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
21. Q: How can summarization hallucinations and factual distortion be reduced?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends explicitly asking for a factual summary without adding new information, reducing sampling, and reviewing the output against the source. Additional controls include using a fixed source document, preserving key quantities and qualifications, requesting citations or source spans where supported, checking names and dates, and routing uncertain summaries to a human reviewer. Lower-randomness decoding can reduce variation but cannot guarantee that the output is correct. Automated checks can identify changed numbers or missing terms, but semantic fidelity often requires human judgment. A reliable workflow records the source version, prompt version, model configuration, reviewer, corrections, and approval status.
Study instruction:
- Create a summary verification checklist for facts, numbers, dates, qualifications, omissions, and unsupported additions.
Key point:
- Constrained prompts and deterministic settings can help, but do not replace verification.
- High-impact summaries need traceability and review.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
22. Q: How does dialogue generation work with a generative LLM?
A: Show answer
Dialogue generation uses a prompt that establishes a conversation context, speaker roles, and an initial turn. The model then generates a continuation that may answer a question or extend the exchange. The chapter illustrates a renewable-energy conversation with Person A and Person B and shows how the maximum number of new tokens influences the length of the continuation. Dialogue generation can support conversational AI, customer service, and virtual assistants, but a single prompt continuation is not the same as a complete dialogue system. A deployed assistant must preserve state, apply business rules, handle uncertainty, and know when to stop or escalate rather than generating an unbounded conversation.
Study instruction:
- Write a dialogue prompt with explicit speaker roles, objective, context, and a clear stopping or handoff condition.
Key point:
- Dialogue generation produces a conversational continuation from supplied context.
- A real assistant also needs state, controls, and escalation logic.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
23. Q: Why does a chatbot need a dialog management system in addition to an LLM?
A: Show answer
A dialog management system maintains the conversation state, tracks prior turns and relevant user preferences, and coordinates the next action. The LLM generates language, but the surrounding system determines which information is available, what task is in progress, whether a tool or workflow should be invoked, and whether the conversation has reached a valid outcome. Without state management, the chatbot may lose context, repeat questions, or make inconsistent commitments. The chapter recommends interfacing the LLM with a dialog manager and designing conversation flows. The system should separate model-generated text from authoritative transaction state and should not let generated language silently change account records or business rules.
Study instruction:
- Draw a chatbot architecture showing the user interface, dialog state, LLM, business systems, validation, and audit log.
Key point:
- The LLM generates responses; the dialog manager controls conversation state and workflow.
- Authoritative business data should remain outside uncontrolled text generation.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
24. Q: What is the purpose of fallback mechanisms in an LLM-powered chatbot?
A: Show answer
A fallback mechanism provides a safe alternative when the model is uncertain, the request is outside scope, a required system is unavailable, or the issue needs human judgment. The chapter gives human customer support as an example. Fallbacks prevent the chatbot from improvising answers when it lacks adequate evidence or authority. A practical design defines confidence or rule-based triggers, captures the conversation context, informs the user of the handoff, and routes the case to an appropriate person or process. The handoff should preserve relevant information while respecting privacy. A fallback must be tested for availability and should not create a dead end or silently abandon the user.
Study instruction:
- Define fallback triggers for an ambiguous request, a system failure, a high-risk question, and an unsupported task.
Key point:
- Fallbacks prevent unsupported generation from becoming an unsafe decision.
- Escalation must preserve context and reach an accountable handler.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
25. Q: How do personalization, contextual awareness, and tone affect chatbot user experience?
A: Show answer
Personalization adapts responses to relevant user history or preferences; contextual awareness helps the model interpret the current request in light of prior turns; tone and personality make the interaction consistent with the organization and audience. The chapter identifies these as user-experience considerations for dialogue systems. They must be implemented carefully: use only authorized, relevant user information, make the assistant's role clear, and avoid pretending to remember facts that are not available. Tone should not obscure uncertainty or make a risky answer seem more authoritative than it is. Users should be able to correct contextual assumptions and request human assistance when personalization or conversation history is wrong.
Study instruction:
- Design a chatbot response policy that defines permitted personalization data, context retention, tone, and how users can correct mistakes.
Key point:
- Personalization and context can improve relevance and continuity.
- Privacy, transparency, and correction mechanisms are essential safeguards.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
26. Q: What should be monitored after an LLM chatbot is deployed?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends monitoring performance through user satisfaction, resolution rate, and average handling time, and using feedback to identify conversations that frustrate users or go off track. Operational monitoring should also examine response accuracy, unsupported claims, escalation frequency, latency, cost, privacy incidents, and performance across user groups. Metrics need context: a shorter handling time is not necessarily good if the chatbot resolves fewer issues or gives inaccurate answers. Review samples of conversations, document recurring failure modes, and use approved changes to prompts, retrieval sources, or model versions. Ethical and legal compliance, transparency about AI use, and controls against harmful or biased content should be part of ongoing oversight.
Study instruction:
- Build a monitoring dashboard with service, quality, safety, privacy, and cost indicators, and explain how each metric could be misleading in isolation.
Key point:
- Deployment requires continuous monitoring and corrective improvement.
- Service efficiency must be balanced with quality, safety, and user outcomes.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
27. Q: What is zero-shot prompting?
A: Show answer
Zero-shot prompting gives the model an instruction and relevant context without providing solved examples of the requested task. The chapter notes that instruction-tuned models may perform well in such settings. For example, a prompt can ask for sentiment classification or a topic label directly. Zero-shot prompting is efficient and keeps the prompt shorter than a few-shot prompt, but it depends heavily on the model's existing capabilities and interpretation of the instruction. If the task has unusual labels, specialized terminology, or strict output requirements, the model may need examples or further adaptation. Zero-shot results should be measured on representative cases rather than accepted because a few outputs look plausible.
Study instruction:
- Create a zero-shot prompt for a classification task and test it against a labeled set without adding examples.
Key point:
- Zero-shot prompting relies on instructions and context alone.
- It is a baseline, not evidence of consistent performance.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
28. Q: What is few-shot prompting, and how does it improve task guidance?
A: Show answer
Few-shot prompting adds a small number of input-output examples to the prompt. The examples demonstrate the pattern the model should follow, including label names, formatting, and how to handle representative cases. This can improve performance when an instruction alone is too vague or when the task contains specific nuances. The chapter gives a principle-to-name example and describes use cases in customer service, code generation, and marketing personalization. Few-shot prompting does not update the model's parameters; it conditions the response using the examples in the current context. Examples should be selected carefully and evaluated on unseen cases to avoid overfitting the prompt to a narrow pattern.
Study instruction:
- Add two or three representative demonstrations to a zero-shot prompt and compare results on a separate test set.
Key point:
- Few-shot examples communicate the desired task pattern in context.
- The model's parameters remain unchanged during prompting.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
29. Q: What are the main limitations of few-shot prompting?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies several limitations: a model may still struggle with complex reasoning, examples increase prompt length and computational cost, and the context window limits how many examples can be included. The model may also infer unintended patterns from example order, phrasing, or label distribution. Poor demonstrations can therefore make performance worse rather than better. To reduce these risks, select diverse examples, balance categories, vary ordering during tests, and measure performance on held-out cases. Few-shot prompts should be versioned and kept concise enough to leave room for the actual user input and output. For tasks requiring stable behavior across many requests, fine-tuning or a specialist model may be more appropriate.
Study instruction:
- Test whether a few-shot classifier changes when examples are reordered, removed, or replaced with a balanced set.
Key point:
- More examples do not automatically mean better prompting.
- Prompt length, example bias, and unintended pattern learning must be controlled.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
30. Q: What is chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting, and what kinds of tasks is it intended to support?
A: Show answer
Chain-of-thought prompting asks a model to work through intermediate steps or provide a structured explanation before presenting an answer. The chapter describes direct instructions such as “consider this step by step” and few-shot examples that demonstrate a sequence of reasoning steps. It is intended for tasks involving multi-stage calculation, scientific analysis, or logical relationships where a direct answer may omit important intermediate considerations. A well-designed prompt can also request a concise rationale, assumptions, and checks. However, a generated explanation should be treated as model output, not as proof that the internal computation followed the same reasoning or that the conclusion is correct.
Study instruction:
- Use a multi-step problem and compare a direct-answer prompt with a prompt requesting a structured explanation and final verification.
Key point:
- CoT prompting encourages explicit intermediate steps in the response.
- The explanation still needs independent checking.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
31. Q: How can chain-of-thought prompting support error analysis and learning?
A: Show answer
A structured explanation can help a reviewer see which assumptions, calculations, or intermediate conclusions the model chose to present. This can make it easier to identify an obvious arithmetic error, a missing step, or a misunderstood instruction. The chapter highlights educational value, user trust, and error analysis as potential benefits. Those benefits depend on checking the explanation against the problem and reliable methods. A plausible rationale can be incomplete or post hoc, so it should not be treated as a faithful record of hidden internal computation. In high-stakes use, require verifiable intermediate outputs, citations, calculations, or external checks rather than relying on persuasive prose alone.
Study instruction:
- Review a model-generated explanation and independently verify each factual premise and calculation before accepting its conclusion.
Key point:
- Explanations can help reviewers inspect a response.
- A convincing rationale is not guaranteed to be faithful or correct.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
32. Q: When should prompting be preferred over fine-tuning?
A: Show answer
Prompting is often preferable when the task is close to the model's existing capabilities, a quick prototype is needed, labeled data is scarce, requirements are changing, or additional training resources are unavailable. It avoids a parameter-update process and can be deployed by changing the input instructions. The chapter notes that prompting offers computational efficiency and quick experimentation but limited customization. It may not adequately capture specialized jargon, low-resource languages, or a domain far from the model's pre-training. The decision should consider performance requirements, privacy, cost, time to market, data availability, and how consistently the model must behave across many inputs.
Study instruction:
- Apply the decision criteria to a new use case and document why prompting is sufficient or why its limitations require adaptation.
Key point:
- Prompting is fast and resource-light when the base model already fits the task.
- Specialized or strict tasks may exceed what prompting can reliably achieve.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
33. Q: When is fine-tuning more appropriate than prompt engineering?
A: Show answer
Fine-tuning may be appropriate when the target domain differs substantially from the model's training experience, the application requires a less-supported language, the output must follow a stable task-specific pattern, or prompt refinement has not reached the required performance. It may also be considered when privacy or infrastructure requirements favor an organization-controlled model environment. Fine-tuning requires a suitable labeled dataset, computational resources, technical expertise, and ongoing evaluation. It is not automatically more accurate than prompting. The chapter's decision framework asks teams to assess task alignment, data availability, performance needs, resources, and time to market before choosing the approach.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a prompting-versus-fine-tuning decision record for a domain-specific task, including data, performance, cost, privacy, and maintenance evidence.
Key point:
- Fine-tuning offers deeper customization but requires data and resources.
- Choose it only when measured benefits justify the additional complexity.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
34. Q: What is fine-tuning, and how does it adapt a pre-trained LLM to a specific task?
A: Show answer
Fine-tuning continues training from a pre-trained model using a curated dataset for a target task or domain. The model's general language representations provide a starting point, while the new examples adjust its behavior toward the task's vocabulary, labels, response format, or domain conventions. The chapter describes applications such as sentiment classification and question answering. Fine-tuning can use fewer labeled examples than training from scratch, but the dataset must still represent the intended use. Poor labels, narrow coverage, excessive training, or an inappropriate learning rate can cause overfitting or bias. A separate evaluation set and documented model configuration are necessary to establish whether adaptation improved the target task.
Study instruction:
- Explain what is inherited from pre-training, what is learned from the task dataset, and how a held-out set tests the result.
Key point:
- Fine-tuning specializes a pre-trained model using task-specific data.
- Data quality and independent evaluation determine whether specialization is useful.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
35. Q: What data requirements should be considered before fine-tuning a model?
A: Show answer
A fine-tuning dataset should be relevant to the intended task, sufficiently diverse, accurately labeled, and representative of the inputs the model will encounter after deployment. It should include edge cases, ambiguous examples, and realistic language rather than only easy or idealized records. The chapter's sentiment example recommends balanced positive, negative, and neutral reviews to reduce class bias. For medical question answering, the PubMedQA data includes questions, contexts, long answers, and final yes/no/maybe decisions. Data provenance, licensing, privacy, duplicates, annotation consistency, and possible leakage must be checked. A large dataset is not automatically good if its labels are unreliable or its distribution differs from production use.
Study instruction:
- Create a dataset readiness checklist covering provenance, consent or lawful basis, label quality, balance, duplicates, domain coverage, and split integrity.
Key point:
- Fine-tuning quality depends on representative and trustworthy data.
- Data governance is part of model development, not an afterthought.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
36. Q: How should a sentiment-analysis fine-tuning project be structured?
A: Show answer
The chapter's customer-review case begins with a clear objective: classify reviews as positive, negative, or neutral. The team selects a pre-trained model and collects labeled reviews that represent the platform's typical language. It then preprocesses the text, adapts the model using the labeled data, and evaluates it on a separate set. The chapter recommends monitoring training to prevent overfitting and using precision, recall, F1, and accuracy. A robust project also defines annotation rules for sarcasm, mixed sentiment, slang, and aspect-level opinions. It records the dataset and model versions and reviews errors by class and customer segment before deployment.
Study instruction:
- Design a sentiment fine-tuning plan with a label guide, representative data split, model checkpoint, training controls, and class-specific evaluation.
Key point:
- Fine-tuning should follow a defined task, curated labels, and held-out evaluation.
- Balanced examples and error analysis reduce avoidable classification bias.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
37. Q: What are common fine-tuning pitfalls, and how can they be mitigated?
A: Show answer
The chapter highlights data scarcity, domain mismatch, and overfitting. When labeled data is limited, teams may consider carefully validated augmentation methods, but synthetic examples must not be mistaken for independent real-world evidence. Domain mismatch can be reduced by selecting a more relevant pre-trained checkpoint or adding domain-specific data. Overfitting can be controlled through early stopping, regularization such as dropout, appropriate learning rates, and validation monitoring. Other important risks include duplicate leakage, label noise, class imbalance, privacy exposure, and catastrophic forgetting. Each mitigation should be tested on a held-out set; increasing training data or epochs without checking its effect can worsen generalization.
Study instruction:
- For a small domain-specific dataset, identify the likely failure modes and choose one measurable mitigation for each.
Key point:
- Data scarcity, mismatch, and overfitting can undermine fine-tuning.
- Mitigations must be validated rather than assumed to work.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
38. Q: What is parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT), and why is it useful for large models?
A: Show answer
PEFT adapts a pre-trained model by training only a small subset of parameters or adding a compact set of trainable parameters while keeping most base weights fixed. The chapter contrasts this with full fine-tuning, which updates the entire network and requires memory for weights, gradients, optimizer states, and activations. PEFT can reduce compute and storage costs, make task-specific adapters portable, and allow one base model to support several tasks without storing a complete full-sized copy for each one. The chapter names adapter-based, prompt-tuning, and reparameterization-based methods. PEFT still needs representative data, careful configuration, and independent evaluation.
Study instruction:
- Compare full fine-tuning with a PEFT approach by listing which parameters are updated and which artifacts must be stored for each task.
Key point:
- PEFT reduces the number of trainable parameters and task-specific storage.
- Lower resource requirements do not remove data or evaluation requirements.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
39. Q: What is catastrophic forgetting, and how can PEFT help reduce its risk?
A: Show answer
Catastrophic forgetting occurs when adaptation to a new task substantially degrades capabilities learned previously. Full fine-tuning changes many base parameters, so a narrow or biased dataset can shift the model away from earlier behavior. PEFT limits updates to a smaller set of parameters or adapter components, which can reduce the extent of disruption to the frozen base model. This is a risk-reduction strategy, not a guarantee that no previous capability will be affected. Teams should still run regression tests on earlier tasks, monitor out-of-domain performance, and keep the base model and adapter versions separate so that changes can be traced and rolled back.
Study instruction:
- Design regression tests that compare the base model and fine-tuned adapter on both the new task and selected earlier capabilities.
Key point:
- Catastrophic forgetting is degradation of previously learned capabilities during adaptation.
- PEFT can reduce disruption but still requires regression evaluation.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
40. Q: How does LoRA reduce the number of trainable parameters?
A: Show answer
Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) keeps the pre-trained weight matrix frozen and represents the task-specific weight update through smaller low-rank matrices. Instead of updating every element of a large weight matrix, training adjusts the compact matrices that approximate the needed update. The chapter illustrates LoRA as an additional low-rank path alongside the original frozen weights and explains that the adapter can be stored separately from the base model. The method reduces memory and storage requirements and allows the same base model to be reused for multiple tasks. Its effectiveness depends on the selected rank, target modules, scaling, dropout, data, and evaluation results.
Study instruction:
- Draw a frozen base weight path and a low-rank update path, then explain how the two contributions combine in the adapted layer.
Key point:
- LoRA learns compact low-rank updates while keeping base weights fixed.
- Adapter configuration determines the capacity and resource trade-off.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
41. Q: What do the LoRA parameters r, lora_alpha, target_modules, lora_dropout, and bias control?
A: Show answer
The rank r determines the inner dimension of the low-rank update and therefore influences the number of trainable parameters. lora_alpha scales the contribution of the adapter. target_modules specifies which model components receive LoRA updates; the chapter's example targets query and value projections. lora_dropout applies dropout to the adapter path to help reduce overfitting. bias controls whether bias parameters are trained, with options such as none, all, or lora_only. These settings are architecture-dependent and should be checked against the actual model's module names. The chapter's example uses r=16, alpha=32, dropout=0.05, bias none, and a sequence-to-sequence task type.
Study instruction:
- Inspect the model module names and explain how changing rank or target modules would affect trainable parameter count and adaptation capacity.
Key point:
- LoRA settings control adapter capacity, scaling, location, and regularization.
- Configuration must match the model architecture and task.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
42. Q: What is the difference between LoRA and QLoRA?
A: Show answer
LoRA introduces trainable low-rank updates while freezing the base model. QLoRA combines this adapter approach with quantization of the frozen base model to reduce memory further. The chapter describes QLoRA techniques including four-bit NormalFloat quantization, double quantization of quantization constants, and paged optimizer behavior using unified memory to manage transfers between CPU and GPU. These techniques aim to make fine-tuning large models possible with constrained GPU memory. They introduce additional implementation and compatibility considerations. The chapter's worked code uses an int8 loading path and an int8 preparation function, so it should be described precisely as an int8 LoRA-style example rather than assumed to implement every four-bit QLoRA component described conceptually.
Study instruction:
- Compare the conceptual QLoRA method with the chapter's actual int8 code and identify which quantization settings are present or absent.
Key point:
- LoRA reduces trainable parameters; QLoRA also quantizes the frozen base model.
- Distinguish the method described from the exact quantization used in code.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
43. Q: What is quantization, and why is it used when fine-tuning or serving large models?
A: Show answer
Quantization represents model weights or activations with fewer bits than the original numerical format, reducing memory requirements and sometimes improving hardware efficiency. The chapter discusses int8 loading and four-bit quantization in the QLoRA context. Lower-bit representations can allow larger models to fit on limited hardware, but quantization may introduce numerical error and hardware-specific behavior. It is therefore a trade-off among memory, speed, accuracy, compatibility, and portability. A quantized model should be tested on the target task and deployment hardware; the assumption that quantization always improves speed or preserves accuracy is unsafe. Record the quantization format and implementation used for every experiment.
Study instruction:
- Compare a full-precision checkpoint with a quantized version on memory use, latency, and task-specific quality.
Key point:
- Quantization reduces numerical precision to save resources.
- Quality and compatibility must be measured on the target system.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
44. Q: What is PubMedQA, and why is it used in the chapter's fine-tuning exercise?
A: Show answer
PubMedQA is a biomedical question-answering dataset built from research abstracts. The chapter describes each example as containing a question, an abstract context, a long answer based on the abstract's conclusion, and a yes/no/maybe decision. It also describes three dataset groups: expert-annotated, unlabeled, and artificially generated instances. The exercise uses the labeled configuration and aims to fine-tune FLAN-T5-XXL to produce longer answers grounded in the provided context. The dataset is useful for demonstrating domain-specific QA, but its labels and abstracts do not make a model a clinical decision-maker. Output must remain tied to the source evidence and be reviewed by qualified experts for medical use.
Study instruction:
- Identify the role of question, context, long_answer, and final_decision fields and explain which field is used as the generation target.
Key point:
- PubMedQA provides biomedical questions and evidence contexts for QA research.
- A benchmark model is not a substitute for clinical judgment.

- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
45. Q: How should the PubMedQA training and test split be constructed safely?
A: Show answer
A valid split must ensure that each example appears in only one partition. The chapter's code shuffles the source training dataset separately when selecting 800 training examples and 200 test examples. Because the two shuffle operations are independent, the selected ranges may overlap; the code does not guarantee disjoint membership. A safer approach is to create one deterministic split once, then assign the resulting partitions to training and testing, recording the random seed and example identifiers. If multiple questions or abstracts are related, group-level splitting may be needed to prevent leakage. The test set should remain untouched during prompt and hyperparameter selection.
Study instruction:
- Replace independent shuffles with a single reproducible split and verify that no record ID occurs in both partitions.
Key point:
- Train and test partitions must be demonstrably disjoint.
- Independent random shuffles can create overlap and invalidate evaluation.

- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
46. Q: Why does the chapter concatenate the PubMedQA context sentences before tokenization?
A: Show answer
The dataset stores context as a dictionary containing multiple sentences, often with section labels such as aims, methods, and results. The chapter joins these sentences into one text passage and retains the question and long answer in separate fields. This creates a simpler input structure for a sequence-to-sequence model: context plus question become the source, and the long answer becomes the target. The transformation should preserve sentence boundaries and section meaning. Joining with punctuation or clear separators helps avoid accidental word concatenation. It is also important to retain source IDs and original sections for traceability, debugging, and later review of whether the generated answer is supported by the evidence.
Study instruction:
- Transform one dataset example and verify that every original context sentence remains present and correctly ordered in the combined passage.
Key point:
- Context concatenation prepares multi-sentence evidence for model input.
- Preserve source structure and identifiers for auditability.

47. Q: What is the role of tokenization in the FLAN-T5 question-answering pipeline?
A: Show answer
Tokenization converts the combined context and question into model-specific token IDs and converts the long answer into target token IDs. The tokenizer handles vocabulary segmentation, special tokens, and sequence formatting expected by FLAN-T5. The chapter loads the tokenizer from the selected model identifier so that preprocessing matches the base model. Input and target sequences are tokenized separately because they have different roles and may need different maximum lengths. Using the wrong tokenizer, inconsistent special tokens, or an incompatible model revision can corrupt the input representation. Preserve the mapping from original text to tokenized records so errors can be traced back to the source example.
Study instruction:
- Tokenize one context-question pair and one answer, then identify source input IDs, target labels, and special-token behavior.
Key point:
- Tokenization converts source and target text into model-compatible sequences.
- The tokenizer must match the model and task configuration.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
48. Q: How should maximum input and output sequence lengths be selected?
A: Show answer
The chapter measures tokenized source and target lengths and uses the 100th percentile as the maximum length, reporting 512 tokens for queries and 171 for responses in its example. A maximum based on the full observed length distribution reduces truncation in that dataset but may increase memory use and padding cost. A lower limit can improve throughput but may remove crucial context or truncate an answer. Length selection should examine percentiles, memory constraints, task relevance, and whether important evidence occurs near the end of long inputs. Record the chosen limits and measure how many examples are truncated. For long contexts, consider structured chunking or retrieval rather than silently discarding evidence.
Study instruction:
- Plot input and target length distributions and compare the proportion of examples truncated at several candidate limits.
Key point:
- Sequence limits balance evidence retention against memory and computation.
- Measure truncation rather than choosing limits blindly.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
49. Q: Why does the preprocessing function combine context and question with an instruction prefix?
A: Show answer
The chapter constructs an input such as “Answer the question based on the context below” followed by the context and question. This instruction tells the sequence-to-sequence model what transformation is expected from the source text. The same instruction pattern must be used at inference time; otherwise, the model receives a different input distribution from the one used during training. The target is the long answer, tokenized separately. The preprocessing function also pads sequences and replaces target padding IDs with -100 so that padding positions are ignored in the loss. The instruction, input format, and label treatment should be documented and tested together.
Study instruction:
- Compare the training input template with the inference prompt and verify that their task instruction and field order match exactly.
Key point:
- Instruction prefixes define the input-output task for the model.
- Training and inference formats must remain consistent.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
50. Q: Why are padding labels replaced with -100 during sequence-to-sequence training?
A: Show answer
Batches often contain sequences of different lengths, so shorter targets are padded to a common length. Those padding positions are not part of the answer the model should learn to generate. Replacing padding token IDs in the labels with -100 tells common loss functions to ignore those positions. This focuses optimization on actual target tokens rather than artificial padding. The data collator also uses -100 as the label padding ID and pads to a multiple of eight in the chapter's example. The tokenizer's pad and end-of-sequence tokens must be configured correctly; changing them without checking the model's expected special-token setup can damage training behavior.
Study instruction:
- Inspect a padded target sequence and verify that only genuine answer tokens contribute to the training loss.
Key point:
- Ignore-index labels exclude padding from the loss calculation.
- Special-token configuration must match the model and tokenizer.
Source links:
- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6
51. Q: Why is FLAN-T5-XXL selected for the PubMedQA long-answer task?
A: Show answer
FLAN-T5-XXL is an instruction-tuned sequence-to-sequence model that accepts text input and generates text output. The chapter selects it because the task requires producing a long answer from a context and question rather than only returning a yes/no/maybe label. The model's instruction-tuning provides a useful starting point, while fine-tuning on PubMedQA long answers is intended to adapt it to biomedical evidence-based responses. The model is very large and resource-intensive, so the exercise uses sharding and quantization techniques. Model size alone does not guarantee a better answer; compare it with smaller alternatives on the same test set, with attention to factual support, latency, and deployment cost.
Study instruction:

Key point:
- FLAN-T5 maps input text to generated output text.

52. Q: What is the purpose of sharding and int8 loading in the chapter's FLAN-T5 setup?
A: Show answer
Sharding distributes a large model's weights across multiple files or device resources so it can be loaded within memory constraints. Int8 loading represents weights in a lower-bit format to reduce memory use compared with full-precision weights. The chapter loads a sharded FLAN-T5-XXL checkpoint with an int8 option and automatic device mapping. These techniques help make a large model accessible on constrained hardware, but they introduce dependencies on library versions, supported hardware, and quantization implementations. They do not eliminate the need to measure memory, speed, and output quality. Document the exact checkpoint, quantization mode, device map, and software versions used in the experiment.
Study instruction:
- Record the base model, sharded checkpoint, quantization mode, and device mapping, then compare actual memory use with the full-precision baseline.
Key point:
- Sharding and quantization address model-loading and memory constraints.
- Hardware and library compatibility must be verified.

54. Q: What does DataCollatorForSeq2Seq do during model training?
A: Show answer
The sequence-to-sequence data collator assembles individual examples into batches and pads source and target sequences to compatible lengths. The chapter sets the label padding ID to -100 so target padding does not contribute to loss and uses pad_to_multiple_of=8 for hardware-friendly alignment. The collator also receives the tokenizer and model so it can follow the model's sequence-to-sequence requirements. It does not correct mislabeled examples, wrong tokenization, or an unsuitable maximum length. Inspect a batch before training to confirm that input IDs, attention masks, labels, and ignored padding positions are aligned as intended.

56. Q: Why is the model's use_cache setting disabled during training and restored for inference?
A: Show answer
The chapter disables model.config.use_cache during training to avoid warnings and re-enables caching for inference. Autoregressive decoding can reuse previously computed key and value states to avoid recomputing the full context at each generated token. During training, the caching behavior may be incompatible with the gradient and memory behavior of the training loop or may be unnecessary. The exact requirements depend on the model architecture and library version. The important operational point is to manage the setting deliberately: record the training configuration, restore the appropriate inference setting, and test generation after loading the saved adapter and base model.
Study instruction:
- Compare training and inference configuration and verify that the saved model can generate outputs after the cache setting is restored.
Key point:
- Cache settings can differ between training and autoregressive inference.
- Validate the final inference configuration after training.

57. Q: How should a LoRA adapter and tokenizer be saved for later inference?
A: Show answer
The chapter saves the PEFT model and tokenizer to a named directory using save_pretrained. A LoRA checkpoint typically stores adapter weights and configuration rather than a complete copy of the large base model. For later inference, the correct base model must be loaded and the adapter attached to it; the matching tokenizer and special-token configuration must also be available. The base model identifier and revision should be recorded so the adapter is not accidentally loaded against an incompatible checkpoint. Saving only an adapter does not make it self-contained. The chapter reports a compact checkpoint of approximately 84 MB, while other summary text gives a rounded figure near 80 MB; actual size should be measured from the saved artifact.
Study instruction:

Key point:
- A LoRA adapter depends on a compatible base model and tokenizer.
- Record all components needed to reproduce inference.

59. Q: Which metrics does the chapter use to evaluate long-answer question generation, and what does each measure?
A: Show answer
The chapter lists F1, BLEU, ROUGE, and sentence similarity. Token-overlap F1 measures precision and recall of shared tokens between a prediction and reference. BLEU evaluates n-gram overlap, traditionally emphasizing precision, while ROUGE measures overlap patterns useful for comparing summaries or generated answers. Sentence similarity uses vector representations and cosine similarity to estimate semantic closeness. These metrics provide different views of textual similarity, but none alone establishes that a medical answer is correct, complete, safe, or fully supported by the abstract. Human expert review and source-grounded factual checks are needed alongside automatic scores, particularly when answers may affect clinical interpretation.
Study instruction:
- Explain what each metric rewards and identify one type of factual error that could still receive a relatively high score.
Key point:
- Overlap and similarity metrics measure aspects of text correspondence.
- They do not replace factual and domain-expert validation.

- Solanki and Khublani, Generative Large Language Models, Chapter 6

61. Q: What limitations arise when using BLEU, ROUGE, F1, or sentence similarity for biomedical long-answer evaluation?
A: Show answer
Reference-based metrics compare a generated answer with one or more reference answers, but a valid biomedical answer may use different wording from the reference. Conversely, a generated answer can share many words with the reference while misstating a dosage, population, causal relationship, or qualification. Sentence embeddings can capture broad semantic similarity but may miss a critical negation or numerical discrepancy. The chapter's example output repeats some statements, illustrating why fluency and similarity should be reviewed alongside factual support. A stronger evaluation combines automatic metrics with expert scoring of answerability, evidence attribution, completeness, contradictions, unsupported claims, and safety-relevant omissions.
Study instruction:
- Design a human evaluation rubric that scores factual support, completeness, uncertainty, and critical numerical accuracy in addition to automatic metrics.
Key point:
- Text similarity is not the same as domain correctness.
- Medical QA requires source-grounded and expert evaluation.

63. Q: What governance and safety controls are needed before deploying an LLM fine-tuned on biomedical research?
A: Show answer
A biomedical QA model should be bounded to an intended use, trained only on data that can be lawfully and safely processed, and evaluated on representative research questions. Outputs should cite or point to supporting abstract passages where possible, preserve uncertainty, and avoid converting research evidence into patient-specific medical advice. Access, logging, retention, and model updates should follow applicable privacy and institutional requirements. Qualified reviewers should assess consequential answers, and the system should have an escalation path for ambiguous, unsupported, or high-risk questions. 1. Q: What advanced techniques for large language models are examined in this chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter explores two related ways to adapt large language models: supervised fine-tuning for abstractive summarization and feedback-driven optimization for controlled text generation. It compares an encoder-decoder T5-small model with a decoder-only Llama-2 chat model, then demonstrates parameter-efficient adaptation through LoRA and 4-bit QLoRA. It also explains the role of reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF), reward models, and Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO). The practical experiment uses GPT-2 and a Yelp sentiment classifier to encourage positive review continuations. These examples are learning exercises, not universal deployment recipes. Their data, model checkpoints, software versions, evaluation methods, and ethical implications must be checked before reuse in a different task or production environment.
Study instruction:
- Map each experiment to its objective, architecture, dataset, adaptation method, evaluation signal, and main limitation.
Key point:
- SFT teaches behavior from examples; preference optimization steers behavior using reward signals.
- Experimental settings require independent validation before being treated as production defaults.

2. Q: What is abstractive summarization, and how does it differ from extractive summarization?
A: Show answer
Abstractive summarization creates a shorter text that expresses the source's central meaning in newly generated language. The model must identify salient information, connect ideas, compress details, and paraphrase without changing the facts. Extractive summarization instead selects existing sentences or phrases from the source. Extractive output is easier to trace to the document but can be repetitive or poorly connected. Abstractive output can be more coherent and concise, but it can also invent details, merge unrelated statements, or omit qualifications. The appropriate method depends on the task: a legal or audit summary may require strong traceability, while a news brief may benefit from synthesis. Neither method removes the need to verify that the summary accurately represents the source.
Study instruction:
- Produce both an extractive and an abstractive summary of one article and trace each abstractive claim back to source evidence.
Key point:
- Extractive methods select source text; abstractive methods generate a new formulation of the source meaning.
- Fluency and brevity do not prove factual faithfulness.
Source links:
- Generative Artificial Intelligence, Chapter 7
- Hugging Face: XSum dataset card
3. Q: Why is the XSum dataset suitable for the chapter's abstractive summarization experiments?
A: Show answer
XSum pairs news articles with short, professionally written summaries and is designed for highly compressed, abstractive news summarization. The chapter uses the document field as the model input and the summary field as the target. The dataset card describes separate training, validation, and test splits, with approximately 204,045 training examples, 11,332 validation examples, and 11,334 test examples. The chapter also analyzes word counts before choosing token limits; it reports mean and median document lengths of roughly 374 and 295 words, and summary lengths of about 21 words. These figures describe the chapter's inspected data, not token counts. XSum's news domain and editorial conventions may not represent legal, clinical, financial, or organizational documents, so domain transfer must be evaluated separately.
Study instruction:
- Inspect several XSum pairs and compare the source facts retained, omitted, and paraphrased in each reference summary.
Key point:
- XSum supports concise abstractive news summarization and provides distinct evaluation splits.
- Results on news data do not establish performance in other domains.
